I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.

But if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF48.1, F33.0, F33.1, F06.0, F06.9, F21, F19.2, F10.2, F04, F54\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I keep losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments or trying to set up the little lobby displays—we do seasonal ones, and I was fussing with this brochure stand the other day—and then my mind just sort of slips off track. I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked, and then I realize I don’t know where I was. It’s not huge, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless, no… I mean, I still like the things I usually like, I think. Sometimes I get worn out and then I don’t feel like doing much, but that could just be the day, you know? Like after work, if the phone’s been ringing nonstop, I’ll just sit there and not even pick a show, and then my husband will ask what I want for dinner and I realize I’ve been staring at the counter. But down, like sad all the time… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or where you feel more down or flat than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate that out. I mean, I have days where I’m just kind of… blah, I guess, and I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. Like sometimes I’ll plan to sit down and watch one of those baking shows I like, and then I end up scrolling through recipes instead, and then somehow I’m reading reviews for measuring cups, which is ridiculous because we already have three sets.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change, like things I used to enjoy suddenly don’t matter to me. It’s more just some days I’m tired or not really in the mood, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know it might not make sense or there’s not much you can do about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because sometimes I know I’m making too much of something, but I still can’t leave it alone. I’ll worry about the work schedule, or whether I entered something wrong, or if a bill is going to come through at the wrong time.\n\nEven little paperwork mistakes get stuck in my head, like one small mix-up is going to snowball into some much bigger problem. And then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m trying to do something else, like making travel plans or even just organizing things at the front desk, and suddenly I’ve gone down this whole rabbit hole.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or have a low mood most days, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: Most days? I don’t think so… I mean, I have off days, definitely, but I wouldn’t call it a low mood most days. Sometimes I’m just tired or kind of mentally cluttered, if that makes sense.\n\nLike yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for a coupon I thought I had, and then I was annoyed at myself, and then I’m standing there thinking, why am I like this over a coupon? But that’s not really the same as feeling down all day. So… no, I don’t think it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you’ve had enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.

I’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.

Like at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.

I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.

Like if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.

My sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.

But down, like sad all the time… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or where you feel more down or flat than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate that out. I mean, I have days where I’m just kind of… blah, I guess, and I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. Like sometimes I’ll plan to sit down and watch one of those baking shows I like, and then I end up scrolling through recipes instead, and then somehow I’m reading reviews for measuring cups, which is ridiculous because we already have three sets.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change, like things I used to enjoy suddenly don’t matter to me. It’s more just some days I’m tired or not really in the mood, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know it might not make sense or there’s not much you can do about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because sometimes I know I’m making too much of something, but I still can’t leave it alone. I’ll worry about the work schedule, or whether I entered something wrong, or if a bill is going to come through at the wrong time.\n\nEven little paperwork mistakes get stuck in my head, like one small mix-up is going to snowball into some much bigger problem. And then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m trying to do something else, like making travel plans or even just organizing things at the front desk, and suddenly I’ve gone down this whole rabbit hole.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or have a low mood most days, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: Most days? I don’t think so… I mean, I have off days, definitely, but I wouldn’t call it a low mood most days. Sometimes I’m just tired or kind of mentally cluttered, if that makes sense.\n\nLike yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for a coupon I thought I had, and then I was annoyed at myself, and then I’m standing there thinking, why am I like this over a coupon? But that’s not really the same as feeling down all day. So… no, I don’t think it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you’ve had enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, like it happens even when you don’t want it to or you can’t seem to stop it even if you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already checked it,” but the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it won’t even stay on the one thing. It’ll start with, say, did I write down the right appointment time, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I forgot to return a call, and then whether some detail at home is going to cause a problem. I end up checking things in my head over and over, like that’s going to make it feel settled, but it doesn’t really.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even if it's just in the background?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.\n\nI’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.\n\nLike at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came out of nowhere, like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you felt like something terrible was going to happen right then?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.\n\nLike if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.\n\nMy sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes, I think so. Even when I can’t point to one exact thing, I still feel kind of… braced, I guess.

Like at the front desk, I can be smiling and answering phones and helping people find where they need to go, and on the outside I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.

But down, like sad all the time… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or where you feel more down or flat than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate that out. I mean, I have days where I’m just kind of… blah, I guess, and I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. Like sometimes I’ll plan to sit down and watch one of those baking shows I like, and then I end up scrolling through recipes instead, and then somehow I’m reading reviews for measuring cups, which is ridiculous because we already have three sets.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change, like things I used to enjoy suddenly don’t matter to me. It’s more just some days I’m tired or not really in the mood, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know it might not make sense or there’s not much you can do about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because sometimes I know I’m making too much of something, but I still can’t leave it alone. I’ll worry about the work schedule, or whether I entered something wrong, or if a bill is going to come through at the wrong time.\n\nEven little paperwork mistakes get stuck in my head, like one small mix-up is going to snowball into some much bigger problem. And then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m trying to do something else, like making travel plans or even just organizing things at the front desk, and suddenly I’ve gone down this whole rabbit hole.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or have a low mood most days, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: Most days? I don’t think so… I mean, I have off days, definitely, but I wouldn’t call it a low mood most days. Sometimes I’m just tired or kind of mentally cluttered, if that makes sense.\n\nLike yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for a coupon I thought I had, and then I was annoyed at myself, and then I’m standing there thinking, why am I like this over a coupon? But that’s not really the same as feeling down all day. So… no, I don’t think it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you’ve had enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, like it happens even when you don’t want it to or you can’t seem to stop it even if you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already checked it,” but the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it won’t even stay on the one thing. It’ll start with, say, did I write down the right appointment time, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I forgot to return a call, and then whether some detail at home is going to cause a problem. I end up checking things in my head over and over, like that’s going to make it feel settled, but it doesn’t really.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even if it's just in the background?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.\n\nI’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.\n\nLike at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came out of nowhere, like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you felt like something terrible was going to happen right then?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.\n\nLike if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.\n\nMy sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or specific problem to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I can’t point to one exact thing, I still feel kind of… braced, I guess.\n\nLike at the front desk, I can be smiling and answering phones and helping people find where they need to go, and on the outside I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nIt’s funny, because sometimes nothing is happening at all—just the normal lobby noise, people coming in and out—and I’ll realize I’m sitting there like I’m expecting some problem to pop up. So yes, even without a clear reason, that feeling can still be there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, because if I’m really distracted by something nice, like watching a show or talking to someone, it can fade back a little.

But it does follow me more than I’d like. Even ordinary things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, paperwork—my mind kind of grabs onto them and starts going, “What if this one small thing turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little mix-up on a form at work, and suddenly I’m thinking about all the ways it could cause trouble later.

And then sometimes I’m at home, supposedly relaxing, and I’ll remember some tiny thing I need to check, or wonder if I paid something, and there goes the relaxing. Sorry, I’m rambling a bit—but yes, it’s kind of there a lot, even when there isn’t really a big reason.

I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked, and then I realize I don’t know where I was. It’s not huge, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless, no… I mean, I still like the things I usually like, I think. Sometimes I get worn out and then I don’t feel like doing much, but that could just be the day, you know? Like after work, if the phone’s been ringing nonstop, I’ll just sit there and not even pick a show, and then my husband will ask what I want for dinner and I realize I’ve been staring at the counter. But down, like sad all the time… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or where you feel more down or flat than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate that out. I mean, I have days where I’m just kind of… blah, I guess, and I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. Like sometimes I’ll plan to sit down and watch one of those baking shows I like, and then I end up scrolling through recipes instead, and then somehow I’m reading reviews for measuring cups, which is ridiculous because we already have three sets.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change, like things I used to enjoy suddenly don’t matter to me. It’s more just some days I’m tired or not really in the mood, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know it might not make sense or there’s not much you can do about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because sometimes I know I’m making too much of something, but I still can’t leave it alone. I’ll worry about the work schedule, or whether I entered something wrong, or if a bill is going to come through at the wrong time.\n\nEven little paperwork mistakes get stuck in my head, like one small mix-up is going to snowball into some much bigger problem. And then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m trying to do something else, like making travel plans or even just organizing things at the front desk, and suddenly I’ve gone down this whole rabbit hole.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or have a low mood most days, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: Most days? I don’t think so… I mean, I have off days, definitely, but I wouldn’t call it a low mood most days. Sometimes I’m just tired or kind of mentally cluttered, if that makes sense.\n\nLike yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for a coupon I thought I had, and then I was annoyed at myself, and then I’m standing there thinking, why am I like this over a coupon? But that’s not really the same as feeling down all day. So… no, I don’t think it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you’ve had enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, like it happens even when you don’t want it to or you can’t seem to stop it even if you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already checked it,” but the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it won’t even stay on the one thing. It’ll start with, say, did I write down the right appointment time, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I forgot to return a call, and then whether some detail at home is going to cause a problem. I end up checking things in my head over and over, like that’s going to make it feel settled, but it doesn’t really.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even if it's just in the background?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.\n\nI’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.\n\nLike at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came out of nowhere, like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you felt like something terrible was going to happen right then?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.\n\nLike if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.\n\nMy sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or specific problem to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. So yes, even without a clear reason, that feeling can still be there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety or worry is something you feel all the time, even in situations where there’s no real reason for it, like when you’re relaxing at home or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, because if I’m really distracted by something nice, like watching a show or talking to someone, it can fade back a little.\n\nBut it does follow me more than I’d like. Even ordinary things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, paperwork—my mind kind of grabs onto them and starts going, “What if this one small thing turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little mix-up on a form at work, and suddenly I’m thinking about all the ways it could cause trouble later.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’m at home, supposedly relaxing, and I’ll remember some tiny thing I need to check, or wonder if I paid something, and there goes the relaxing. Sorry, I’m rambling a bit—but yes, it’s kind of there a lot, even when there isn’t really a big reason.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your anxiety or worry feels impossible to control, even when you try to stop thinking about it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 44 / receptionist_or_information_clerk / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Uncontrollable_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I can tell myself, ‘Sunny, it’s handled,’ but the thought keeps looping, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another until I’m checking details in my head over and over.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s probably the part that frustrates me the most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and I’ll mean it. But the thought just keeps looping anyway.

And then it doesn’t even stay on the one thing. I’ll start with, say, one concern, and pretty soon my mind has hopped to three other things, and I’m going back over details in my head like I’m trying to find the mistake before it happens.

It’s not like I don’t try to stop. I do. I’ll tell myself to let it go, and then five minutes later I’m right back checking it in my head again.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I keep losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments or trying to set up the little lobby displays—we do seasonal ones, and I was fussing with this brochure stand the other day—and then my mind just sort of slips off track. I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked, and then I realize I don’t know where I was. It’s not huge, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless, no… I mean, I still like the things I usually like, I think. Sometimes I get worn out and then I don’t feel like doing much, but that could just be the day, you know? Like after work, if the phone’s been ringing nonstop, I’ll just sit there and not even pick a show, and then my husband will ask what I want for dinner and I realize I’ve been staring at the counter. But down, like sad all the time… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or where you feel more down or flat than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate that out. I mean, I have days where I’m just kind of… blah, I guess, and I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. Like sometimes I’ll plan to sit down and watch one of those baking shows I like, and then I end up scrolling through recipes instead, and then somehow I’m reading reviews for measuring cups, which is ridiculous because we already have three sets.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change, like things I used to enjoy suddenly don’t matter to me. It’s more just some days I’m tired or not really in the mood, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know it might not make sense or there’s not much you can do about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because sometimes I know I’m making too much of something, but I still can’t leave it alone. I’ll worry about the work schedule, or whether I entered something wrong, or if a bill is going to come through at the wrong time.\n\nEven little paperwork mistakes get stuck in my head, like one small mix-up is going to snowball into some much bigger problem. And then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m trying to do something else, like making travel plans or even just organizing things at the front desk, and suddenly I’ve gone down this whole rabbit hole.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or have a low mood most days, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: Most days? I don’t think so… I mean, I have off days, definitely, but I wouldn’t call it a low mood most days. Sometimes I’m just tired or kind of mentally cluttered, if that makes sense.\n\nLike yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for a coupon I thought I had, and then I was annoyed at myself, and then I’m standing there thinking, why am I like this over a coupon? But that’s not really the same as feeling down all day. So… no, I don’t think it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you’ve had enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, like it happens even when you don’t want it to or you can’t seem to stop it even if you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already checked it,” but the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it won’t even stay on the one thing. It’ll start with, say, did I write down the right appointment time, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I forgot to return a call, and then whether some detail at home is going to cause a problem. I end up checking things in my head over and over, like that’s going to make it feel settled, but it doesn’t really.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even if it's just in the background?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.\n\nI’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.\n\nLike at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came out of nowhere, like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you felt like something terrible was going to happen right then?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.\n\nLike if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.\n\nMy sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or specific problem to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even ordinary things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, paperwork—my mind kind of grabs onto them and starts going, “What if this one small thing turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little mix-up on a form at work, and suddenly I’m thinking about all the ways it could cause trouble later.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’m at home, supposedly relaxing, and I’ll remember some tiny thing I need to check, or wonder if I paid something, and there goes the relaxing. Sorry, I’m rambling a bit—but yes, it’s kind of there a lot, even when there isn’t really a big reason.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety or worry feels impossible to control, even when you try to stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that frustrates me the most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and I’ll mean it. But the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it doesn’t even stay on the one thing. I’ll start with, say, one concern, and pretty soon my mind has hopped to three other things, and I’m going back over details in my head like I’m trying to find the mistake before it happens.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t try to stop. I do. I’ll tell myself to let it go, and then five minutes later I’m right back checking it in my head again.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any physical symptoms, like headaches, stomach issues, or other bodily discomforts, that seem to happen alongside your worry or stress, even when there doesn’t seem to be a clear medical cause?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I notice it more as tension than anything specific like… I don’t know, I don’t want to overstate it.

It’s like when I’m at the front desk, I can still smile and answer questions and point people where they need to go—people probably think I’m totally fine—but inside I feel wound really tight. My shoulders get all raised up, like they’re practically around my ears, and I’m just sitting there waiting for something to go wrong.

And sometimes nothing is actually wrong. It’s just the normal phones, people coming in, someone asking where an office is—oh, and people always ask for the department that moved two years ago, which is a whole thing—but the feeling is there anyway. So yes, I guess physically it shows up as that tight, braced feeling.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF48.1, F33.0, F33.1, F06.0, F06.9, F21, F19.2, F10.2, F04, F54\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I keep losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments or trying to set up the little lobby displays—we do seasonal ones, and I was fussing with this brochure stand the other day—and then my mind just sort of slips off track. I’m thinking about what I might’ve forgotten or overlooked, and then I realize I don’t know where I was. It’s not huge, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say hopeless, no… I mean, I still like the things I usually like, I think. Sometimes I get worn out and then I don’t feel like doing much, but that could just be the day, you know? Like after work, if the phone’s been ringing nonstop, I’ll just sit there and not even pick a show, and then my husband will ask what I want for dinner and I realize I’ve been staring at the counter. But down, like sad all the time… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or where you feel more down or flat than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate that out. I mean, I have days where I’m just kind of… blah, I guess, and I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. Like sometimes I’ll plan to sit down and watch one of those baking shows I like, and then I end up scrolling through recipes instead, and then somehow I’m reading reviews for measuring cups, which is ridiculous because we already have three sets.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change, like things I used to enjoy suddenly don’t matter to me. It’s more just some days I’m tired or not really in the mood, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know it might not make sense or there’s not much you can do about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because sometimes I know I’m making too much of something, but I still can’t leave it alone. I’ll worry about the work schedule, or whether I entered something wrong, or if a bill is going to come through at the wrong time.\n\nEven little paperwork mistakes get stuck in my head, like one small mix-up is going to snowball into some much bigger problem. And then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m trying to do something else, like making travel plans or even just organizing things at the front desk, and suddenly I’ve gone down this whole rabbit hole.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or have a low mood most days, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: Most days? I don’t think so… I mean, I have off days, definitely, but I wouldn’t call it a low mood most days. Sometimes I’m just tired or kind of mentally cluttered, if that makes sense.\n\nLike yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for a coupon I thought I had, and then I was annoyed at myself, and then I’m standing there thinking, why am I like this over a coupon? But that’s not really the same as feeling down all day. So… no, I don’t think it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you’ve had enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, like it happens even when you don’t want it to or you can’t seem to stop it even if you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already checked it,” but the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it won’t even stay on the one thing. It’ll start with, say, did I write down the right appointment time, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I forgot to return a call, and then whether some detail at home is going to cause a problem. I end up checking things in my head over and over, like that’s going to make it feel settled, but it doesn’t really.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even if it's just in the background?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.\n\nI’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.\n\nLike at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came out of nowhere, like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you felt like something terrible was going to happen right then?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.\n\nLike if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.\n\nMy sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or specific problem to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I can’t point to one exact thing, I still feel kind of… braced, I guess.\n\nLike at the front desk, I can be smiling and answering phones and helping people find where they need to go, and on the outside I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nIt’s funny, because sometimes nothing is happening at all—just the normal lobby noise, people coming in and out—and I’ll realize I’m sitting there like I’m expecting some problem to pop up. So yes, even without a clear reason, that feeling can still be there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety or worry is something you feel all the time, even in situations where there’s no real reason for it, like when you’re relaxing at home or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, because if I’m really distracted by something nice, like watching a show or talking to someone, it can fade back a little.\n\nBut it does follow me more than I’d like. Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of worn out or not very motivated, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling “down” for several days.

Like after work, if the phone’s been ringing nonstop, I’ll just sit there and not even pick a show, and then my husband will ask what I want for dinner and I realize I’ve been staring at the counter. But down, like sad all the time… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or where you feel more down or flat than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate that out. I mean, I have days where I’m just kind of… blah, I guess, and I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. Like sometimes I’ll plan to sit down and watch one of those baking shows I like, and then I end up scrolling through recipes instead, and then somehow I’m reading reviews for measuring cups, which is ridiculous because we already have three sets.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change, like things I used to enjoy suddenly don’t matter to me. It’s more just some days I’m tired or not really in the mood, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when you know it might not make sense or there’s not much you can do about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because sometimes I know I’m making too much of something, but I still can’t leave it alone. And then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m trying to do something else, like making travel plans or even just organizing things at the front desk, and suddenly I’ve gone down this whole rabbit hole.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or have a low mood most days, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: Most days? I don’t think so… I mean, I have off days, definitely, but I wouldn’t call it a low mood most days. Sometimes I’m just tired or kind of mentally cluttered, if that makes sense.\n\nLike yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for a coupon I thought I had, and then I was annoyed at myself, and then I’m standing there thinking, why am I like this over a coupon? But that’s not really the same as feeling down all day. So… no, I don’t think it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or like you don’t have much energy, even if you’ve had enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do get tired sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, like it happens even when you don’t want it to or you can’t seem to stop it even if you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already checked it,” but the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it won’t even stay on the one thing. It’ll start with, say, did I write down the right appointment time, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I forgot to return a call, and then whether some detail at home is going to cause a problem. I end up checking things in my head over and over, like that’s going to make it feel settled, but it doesn’t really.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even if it's just in the background?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.\n\nI’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.\n\nLike at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came out of nowhere, like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you felt like something terrible was going to happen right then?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.\n\nLike if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.\n\nMy sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or specific problem to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I can’t point to one exact thing, I still feel kind of… braced, I guess.\n\nLike at the front desk, I can be smiling and answering phones and helping people find where they need to go, and on the outside I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nIt’s funny, because sometimes nothing is happening at all—just the normal lobby noise, people coming in and out—and I’ll realize I’m sitting there like I’m expecting some problem to pop up. So yes, even without a clear reason, that feeling can still be there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety or worry is something you feel all the time, even in situations where there’s no real reason for it, like when you’re relaxing at home or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, because if I’m really distracted by something nice, like watching a show or talking to someone, it can fade back a little.\n\nBut it does follow me more than I’d like. Even ordinary things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, paperwork—my mind kind of grabs onto them and starts going, “What if this one small thing turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little mix-up on a form at work, and suddenly I’m thinking about all the ways it could cause trouble later.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’m at home, supposedly relaxing, and I’ll remember some tiny thing I need to check, or wonder if I paid something, and there goes the relaxing. Sorry, I’m rambling a bit—but yes, it’s kind of there a lot, even when there isn’t really a big reason.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety or worry feels impossible to control, even when you try to stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that frustrates me the most. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything.

But some things take me longer now, especially scheduling. I’ll put something in and then I’ll check it again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I put that in the right slot?” and check it again. And at the front desk, you know, if the phone rings while someone is standing there asking a question, it’s like my brain keeps trying to keep all the pieces in place.

I’ve also had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. Which sounds silly, because it’s just displays, but then I’ll step back and realize I mixed something up or forgot what I was doing halfway through.

So yes, I guess it affects things, but I’m still showing up and getting through the day.

Some days after work I feel sort of drained, but then again I sit at the front desk all day and somehow that can be tiring even though you’re not really moving around much. People think sitting is relaxing, but it’s like… there’s always a phone ringing or someone asking where they’re supposed to go.\n\nBut if I’ve slept okay, I wouldn’t say I’m walking around exhausted all the time. It’s more like certain days I feel worn out, and other days I’m fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, like it happens even when you don’t want it to or you can’t seem to stop it even if you try?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already checked it,” but the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it won’t even stay on the one thing. It’ll start with, say, did I write down the right appointment time, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I forgot to return a call, and then whether some detail at home is going to cause a problem. I end up checking things in my head over and over, like that’s going to make it feel settled, but it doesn’t really.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even if it's just in the background?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not always like a big panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant little background noise.\n\nI’ll be worrying about normal things—my work schedule, or whether a bill got paid, or if I filled out some paperwork wrong. Even travel plans, like if I have to be somewhere at a certain time, I’ll start thinking, well what if one little thing goes wrong and then it turns into this whole mess. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because most of the time it’s just regular life stuff.\n\nLike at work, if I notice a tiny error on a form, I can get stuck thinking it’s going to cause some bigger problem later. And then I’m trying to remind myself, people make small mistakes, it’s fine, but my brain kind of treats it like it’s not fine. So yes, most days there’s something I’m worrying about, even if it’s just humming in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came out of nowhere, like your heart was racing, you couldn't breathe, or you felt like something terrible was going to happen right then?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up or uncomfortable, but “out of nowhere” is hard for me to say, because usually I can trace it back to something I was already thinking about.\n\nLike if I’m running late or I can’t find something important, I might feel really tense, but it’s not like I’m just sitting there and suddenly, bam, I think something terrible is happening. At least I don’t think so.\n\nMy sister had something like that once at a grocery store, actually, where she thought she was going to faint, and then she wouldn’t go back to that store for months. So when you describe it that way, I don’t think that’s really what happens to me. Mine is more… I get wound up, but not sudden like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or specific problem to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I can’t point to one exact thing, I still feel kind of… braced, I guess.\n\nLike at the front desk, I can be smiling and answering phones and helping people find where they need to go, and on the outside I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nIt’s funny, because sometimes nothing is happening at all—just the normal lobby noise, people coming in and out—and I’ll realize I’m sitting there like I’m expecting some problem to pop up. So yes, even without a clear reason, that feeling can still be there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety or worry is something you feel all the time, even in situations where there’s no real reason for it, like when you’re relaxing at home or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, because if I’m really distracted by something nice, like watching a show or talking to someone, it can fade back a little.\n\nBut it does follow me more than I’d like. Even ordinary things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, paperwork—my mind kind of grabs onto them and starts going, “What if this one small thing turns into a whole bigger problem?” Like one little mix-up on a form at work, and suddenly I’m thinking about all the ways it could cause trouble later.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’m at home, supposedly relaxing, and I’ll remember some tiny thing I need to check, or wonder if I paid something, and there goes the relaxing. Sorry, I’m rambling a bit—but yes, it’s kind of there a lot, even when there isn’t really a big reason.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your anxiety or worry feels impossible to control, even when you try to stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that frustrates me the most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and I’ll mean it. But the thought just keeps looping anyway.\n\nAnd then it doesn’t even stay on the one thing. I’ll start with, say, one concern, and pretty soon my mind has hopped to three other things, and I’m going back over details in my head like I’m trying to find the mistake before it happens.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t try to stop. I do. I’ll tell myself to let it go, and then five minutes later I’m right back checking it in my head again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like headaches, stomach issues, or other bodily discomforts, that seem to happen alongside your worry or stress, even when there doesn’t seem to be a clear medical cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I notice it more as tension than anything specific like… I don’t know, I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at the front desk, I can still smile and answer questions and point people where they need to go—people probably think I’m totally fine—but inside I feel wound really tight. My shoulders get all raised up, like they’re practically around my ears, and I’m just sitting there waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd sometimes nothing is actually wrong. It’s just the normal phones, people coming in, someone asking where an office is—oh, and people always ask for the department that moved two years ago, which is a whole thing—but the feeling is there anyway. So yes, I guess physically it shows up as that tight, braced feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods lasting several days where you feel down or lose interest in things, even if it's not very intense, that seem to come and go on their own?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of worn out or not very motivated, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling “down” for several days.\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking, “Oh, I don’t feel like doing anything,” but then it might just be because I’ve had a long day at the desk, or there’s been a weird schedule change, or I got stuck dealing with—sorry, this is off track—but you know how one small thing can kind of throw off the whole day.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of it coming and going on its own. Not in a way I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s definitely happening.”\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety has been affecting your ability to function normally in different areas of your life, like at work, with your family, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything.\n\nBut some things take me longer now, especially scheduling. I’ll put something in and then I’ll check it again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I put that in the right slot?” and check it again. And at the front desk, you know, if the phone rings while someone is standing there asking a question, it’s like my brain keeps trying to keep all the pieces in place.\n\nI’ve also had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. Which sounds silly, because it’s just displays, but then I’ll step back and realize I mixed something up or forgot what I was doing halfway through.\n\nSo yes, I guess it affects things, but I’m still showing up and getting through the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like headaches, stomach issues, or other bodily discomforts, that seem to happen alongside your worry or stress, even when there doesn’t seem to be a clear medical cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I notice it more as tension than anything specific like… I don’t know, I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at the front desk, I can still smile and answer questions and point people where they need to go—people probably think I’m totally fine—but inside I feel wound really tight. My shoulders get all raised up, like they’re practically around my ears, and I’m just sitting there waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nAnd sometimes nothing is actually wrong. It’s just the normal phones, people coming in, someone asking where an office is—oh, and people always ask for the department that moved two years ago, which is a whole thing—but the feeling is there anyway. So yes, I guess physically it shows up as that tight, braced feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods lasting several days where you feel down or lose interest in things, even if it's not very intense, that seem to come and go on their own?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of worn out or not very motivated, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling “down” for several days.\n\nSometimes I’ll start thinking, “Oh, I don’t feel like doing anything,” but then it might just be because I’ve had a long day at the desk, or there’s been a weird schedule change, or I got stuck dealing with—sorry, this is off track—but you know how one small thing can kind of throw off the whole day.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of it coming and going on its own. Not in a way I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s definitely happening.”\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety has been affecting your ability to function normally in different areas of your life, like at work, with your family, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything.\n\nBut some things take me longer now, especially scheduling. I’ll put something in and then I’ll check it again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I put that in the right slot?” and check it again. And at the front desk, you know, if the phone rings while someone is standing there asking a question, it’s like my brain keeps trying to keep all the pieces in place.\n\nI’ve also had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. Which sounds silly, because it’s just displays, but then I’ll step back and realize I mixed something up or forgot what I was doing halfway through.\n\nSo yes, I guess it affects things, but I’m still showing up and getting through the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say there have been times when you felt down or low for several days in a row, even if it wasn’t tied to anything specific happening?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 44 / receptionist_or_information_clerk / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not clearly, anyway. I mean, I’ve had low-energy stretches, like where I just want to go home and not talk to anybody, but then I start wondering if that’s just a normal week being tiring.

And sometimes the weather does that too—like if it’s been gray for three days, I swear everyone at the front desk seems a little flatter, not just me. But I can’t really point to a few days in a row where I felt low for no reason. I haven’t noticed it in that kind of definite way.
